---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F45.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’ve been feeling… strange, I guess. The thing that scares me most is when I’m walking across campus—where I work, and I’ve walked those same paths a million times—it suddenly feels like I’m watching everything through glass. Like I’m not quite inside my own body.\n\nAnd the buildings, the students passing by, all the normal noise between classes… it all feels slightly unreal. I know that sounds odd. I kept thinking maybe I was just tired, or maybe it was the weather—sometimes the light between those big buildings is so harsh—but it’s been frightening enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel like you're watching everything through glass or like you're not quite inside your own body?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly when I’m walking across campus. Not every single second, but often enough that I’ve started almost dreading those walks between buildings.\n\nI’ll be going past students or looking up at the buildings, and suddenly it’s like there’s this pane of glass between me and everything else. Like I’m there, but not quite in my body. I know the campus, I work there, I’ve walked those paths so many times—actually there’s this one walkway near the big lecture hall where the light hits strangely in the afternoon, and I keep wondering if that’s part of it—but it isn’t just the light. The people and buildings feel… slightly unreal. It scares me when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this feeling of being disconnected or things feeling unreal in situations other than walking across campus?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the campus walks. I mean, that’s where I really notice it—the feeling like there’s glass between me and everything, and I’m not fully inside myself.\n\nI suppose I’ve had little moments where I feel… off, like in a hallway or coming out of a building, but it’s hard to separate that from the campus because I’m there so much. I work there, so half my day is walking past students and lecture halls and those big concrete buildings. And then suddenly the whole place looks familiar but also not quite real, like a set or something. That’s the part that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or frightened in situations other than when you're walking across campus?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not exactly the same as the campus thing, but I do get really frightened by sudden noises. Like if a door bangs shut in the hallway, or one of those carts rattles past—our building has these carts people use for supplies, and they make this awful clattering sound on the tile—I jump so hard my chest actually hurts.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because it’s just a door or a cart, and everyone else keeps walking like nothing happened. But I’ll be standing there shaking, and it takes me several minutes to calm down and remember what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected or things feeling unreal is triggered by anything in particular when you're walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure what sets it off. I keep trying to figure it out, because I want there to be something obvious I can avoid.\n\nIt’s mainly just… being out there on campus, walking between buildings, with students moving around me and the buildings sort of looming there. Sometimes I notice the light or the noise or just the way everyone is passing by, and then suddenly it feels like I’m watching it all through glass. Like I’m there, but I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and people look a little unreal.\n\nI don’t know if that’s a trigger exactly. It’s the campus walk itself, I think. That’s what scares me most.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see or hear things that aren't there when you're feeling disconnected or like things are unreal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like seeing a person who isn’t there or hearing voices or anything. It’s more that things look strange, like the campus looks kind of far away or fake, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I second-guess myself because I’ll hear a noise and turn too fast, and then I’m looking around like everyone must have noticed—but that’s usually just an actual door or people talking somewhere nearby. The building carries sound weirdly, too, especially near the stairwells. Sorry, I’m going off track. But no, I don’t think I’m seeing or hearing things that aren’t there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “peak within minutes” sounds very specific, and I don’t know if I’ve been timing it like that.\n\nThere are definitely moments where fear comes on really fast, like my body reacts before I even understand what’s happening. But then I get caught up in trying to figure out if it was the noise, or where I was standing, or whether I’d had coffee that morning—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t know how to tell if it counts as reaching a peak. It feels intense quickly, but I haven’t really measured it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an overwhelming fear that came on very suddenly?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, I think so. It happens really suddenly if there’s a loud noise, like a door banging shut or one of those carts rattling in the hallway. I jump so hard it actually hurts in my chest.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed, because it’s not like anything terrible happened, it’s just a door, you know? But my body doesn’t seem to know that. I’ll be standing there shaking, trying to act normal, and it takes me several minutes before I can get back to whatever I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the sudden fear you feel in response to loud noises, like doors banging or carts rattling, is the same kind of fear you experience when walking across campus?\n\nPatient: Not exactly. The loud noises are more like—my whole body just reacts, like I’ve been startled.\n\nWalking across campus is different, and honestly it scares me more in a weird way. It’s like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m there but not fully in my own body. The buildings and the students around me look… slightly unreal, I guess. Like the campus is normal, people are just going to class, and I’m the one who’s somehow separated from it.\n\nSorry, I know that sounds odd. It’s not just fear like “a door slammed.” It’s more that I feel disconnected, and then that makes me scared.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an overwhelming fear or discomfort that lasted for a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s usually short, but it feels huge when it happens.\n\nLike if a door bangs shut or one of those carts rattles in the hallway, I jump so hard my chest actually hurts. And then I’m shaking, trying to pretend I’m fine, which is ridiculous because everyone else is just walking around like nothing happened. I start thinking about whether they noticed, or whether I look strange, and then I’m not even doing the thing I was supposed to be doing anymore.\n\nIt takes several minutes before I can calm down enough to get back to what I was doing. So… yes, it doesn’t last forever, but in the moment it feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes of intense fear you experience in response to loud noises could be described as panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them panic attacks, exactly. I mean, I hear “panic attack” and I think of something bigger or… I don’t know, something you see in movies.\n\nWhat happens to me is more like my body overreacts before I even understand what happened. If a door bangs or one of those carts rattles in the hallway, I jump so hard my chest hurts, and then I’m shaking for several minutes afterward. I’ll try to go back to what I was doing, but it’s like I have to wait for my body to catch up.\n\nSo maybe? I’m not sure if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of intense fear you experience in response to loud noises happen to you frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes… pretty frequently, I guess. I mean, not every single second, but if I’m in the hallway and a door bangs or one of those carts rattles past, it happens again.\n\nI jump so hard my chest hurts, and then I’m standing there trying to act normal while I’m still shaking. It takes me several minutes before I can really get back to whatever I was doing.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s just a normal hallway noise, you know? People are always moving things around, doors closing, all of that. But my body reacts like it’s something much worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body or the world around you is more distressing to you than the fear you experience in response to loud noises?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. The loud noises scare me, definitely, but that other feeling is worse in a different way.\n\nLike when I’m walking across campus, and I should just be going from one building to another, it suddenly feels like I’m watching everything through glass. The students, the buildings, even my own hands or feet moving—it’s like I’m there but not fully in my own body. And then I start looking around thinking, why does everything seem slightly unreal?\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly about the hallway noises. It’s just… the noise makes me jump, but that disconnected feeling makes me feel like something is really wrong with me.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, is it just what you see that feels unreal, or do sounds or other sensations also feel unreal to you?\n\nPatient: It’s not only what I see, I don’t think. The visual part is the easiest to explain, like everything is behind glass when I’m walking across campus—the buildings, the students passing by, even me moving through it.\n\nBut it’s more like the whole moment feels unreal. Like I’m there, but not fully in my body. I’ll be walking to class or across the quad, and part of me is thinking about something normal, like whether I remembered my notes, and then suddenly everything feels… off. Slightly distant.\n\nThe sounds are there, I guess, but they feel like they’re happening on the other side of that glass too. It scares me because I know I’m supposed to feel present, and I don’t.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, do the sounds around you feel normal?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean… I still hear people talking or footsteps or whatever’s going on around campus, but when that glass feeling happens, it all feels kind of far away, like it’s part of a scene I’m watching instead of something I’m actually in.\n\nWhat scares me most is that I’m walking across campus and the buildings and students look slightly unreal, and even I don’t feel fully in my own body. Then the sounds sort of get pulled into that too, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, do you also feel disconnected from your physical sensations, like touch or smell?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least with my body. It’s hard to separate it out into touch or smell exactly, because when it happens I’m usually just trying to get across campus and not look strange, you know?\n\nBut what scares me most is that I feel like I’m watching everything through glass. Like the buildings and students around me are slightly unreal, and I’m moving through it, but I’m not fully in my own body. I’ll notice my feet walking or my hands holding my bag, but it feels distant somehow.\n\nSmell… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to that part. It’s more the feeling that I’m there but not really there.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, are your emotions also feeling distant or unreal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they do, sort of. It’s hard to explain because I’m usually more focused on how scary the whole thing feels.\n\nWhen I’m walking across campus and it’s like I’m watching everything through glass, the buildings and students look a little unreal, and I don’t feel fully in my own body. And then even my feelings don’t feel quite normal, like they’re happening from farther away.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, I should feel more panic right now, or I should feel more connected to what’s happening, but instead I’m just… floating through it. And then I start worrying that people can tell, which is probably silly because everyone’s rushing to class, and I used to notice that more when I was teaching sections too, how nobody really looks at anyone closely. But in the moment it feels really frightening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're watching everything through glass all the time when you're on campus, or are there moments when it stops and you feel fully present?\n\nPatient: No, not all the time. I mean, campus can set it off, especially when I’m walking between buildings and there are students everywhere, and suddenly it’s that… glass feeling again.\n\nBut there are moments when it eases up. Like if I stop focusing on it, or if I get into a classroom or someone talks to me directly, sometimes I feel more here again. It’s just when it happens, it feels so convincing. The buildings and people look slightly unreal, and I feel like I’m moving through it without being fully in my body.\n\nSorry, I know I keep circling back to that, but that’s the part that scares me most.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But then I get caught up in trying to figure out if it was the noise, or where I was standing, or whether I’d had coffee that morning—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t know how to tell if it counts as reaching a peak. It feels intense quickly, but I haven’t really measured it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an overwhelming fear that came on very suddenly?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, I think so. It happens really suddenly if there’s a loud noise, like a door banging shut or one of those carts rattling in the hallway. I jump so hard it actually hurts in my chest.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed, because it’s not like anything terrible happened, it’s just a door, you know? But my body doesn’t seem to know that. I’ll be standing there shaking, trying to act normal, and it takes me several minutes before I can get back to whatever I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the sudden fear you feel in response to loud noises, like doors banging or carts rattling, is the same kind of fear you experience when walking across campus?\n\nPatient: Not exactly. The loud noises are more like—my whole body just reacts, like I’ve been startled.\n\nWalking across campus is different, and honestly it scares me more in a weird way. It’s like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m there but not fully in my own body. The buildings and the students around me look… slightly unreal, I guess. It’s usually short, but it feels huge when it happens.\n\nLike if a door bangs shut or one of those carts rattles in the hallway, I jump so hard my chest actually hurts. And then I’m shaking, trying to pretend I’m fine, which is ridiculous because everyone else is just walking around like nothing happened. I start thinking about whether they noticed, or whether I look strange, and then I’m not even doing the thing I was supposed to be doing anymore.\n\nIt takes several minutes before I can calm down enough to get back to what I was doing. So… yes, it doesn’t last forever, but in the moment it feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes of intense fear you experience in response to loud noises could be described as panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them panic attacks, exactly. I mean, I hear “panic attack” and I think of something bigger or… I don’t know, something you see in movies.\n\nWhat happens to me is more like my body overreacts before I even understand what happened. If a door bangs or one of those carts rattles in the hallway, I jump so hard my chest hurts, and then I’m shaking for several minutes afterward. I’ll try to go back to what I was doing, but it’s like I have to wait for my body to catch up.\n\nSo maybe? I’m not sure if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of intense fear you experience in response to loud noises happen to you frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes… pretty frequently, I guess. It takes me several minutes before I can really get back to whatever I was doing.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s just a normal hallway noise, you know? People are always moving things around, doors closing, all of that. The loud noises scare me, definitely, but that other feeling is worse in a different way.\n\nLike when I’m walking across campus, and I should just be going from one building to another, it suddenly feels like I’m watching everything through glass. The students, the buildings, even my own hands or feet moving—it’s like I’m there but not fully in my own body. It’s just… the noise makes me jump, but that disconnected feeling makes me feel like something is really wrong with me.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, is it just what you see that feels unreal, or do sounds or other sensations also feel unreal to you?\n\nPatient: It’s not only what I see, I don’t think. The visual part is the easiest to explain, like everything is behind glass when I’m walking across campus—the buildings, the students passing by, even me moving through it.\n\nBut it’s more like the whole moment feels unreal. Like I’m there, but not fully in my body. I’ll be walking to class or across the quad, and part of me is thinking about something normal, like whether I remembered my notes, and then suddenly everything feels… off. Slightly distant.\n\nThe sounds are there, I guess, but they feel like they’re happening on the other side of that glass too. It scares me because I know I’m supposed to feel present, and I don’t.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, do the sounds around you feel normal?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. Then the sounds sort of get pulled into that too, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, do you also feel disconnected from your physical sensations, like touch or smell?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least with my body. It’s hard to separate it out into touch or smell exactly, because when it happens I’m usually just trying to get across campus and not look strange, you know?\n\nBut what scares me most is that I feel like I’m watching everything through glass. And then even my feelings don’t feel quite normal, like they’re happening from farther away.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, I should feel more panic right now, or I should feel more connected to what’s happening, but instead I’m just… floating through it. I work there, so half my day is walking past students and lecture halls and those big concrete buildings. And then suddenly the whole place looks familiar but also not quite real, like a set or something. That’s the part that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or frightened in situations other than when you're walking across campus?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not exactly the same as the campus thing, but I do get really frightened by sudden noises. Like if a door bangs shut in the hallway, or one of those carts rattles past—our building has these carts people use for supplies, and they make this awful clattering sound on the tile—I jump so hard my chest actually hurts.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because it’s just a door or a cart, and everyone else keeps walking like nothing happened. But I’ll be standing there shaking, and it takes me several minutes to calm down and remember what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected or things feeling unreal is triggered by anything in particular when you're walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure what sets it off. I keep trying to figure it out, because I want there to be something obvious I can avoid.\n\nIt’s mainly just… being out there on campus, walking between buildings, with students moving around me and the buildings sort of looming there. Sometimes I notice the light or the noise or just the way everyone is passing by, and then suddenly it feels like I’m watching it all through glass. Like I’m there, but I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and people look a little unreal.\n\nI don’t know if that’s a trigger exactly. It’s the campus walk itself, I think. That’s what scares me most.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see or hear things that aren't there when you're feeling disconnected or like things are unreal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like seeing a person who isn’t there or hearing voices or anything. It’s more that things look strange, like the campus looks kind of far away or fake, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I second-guess myself because I’ll hear a noise and turn too fast, and then I’m looking around like everyone must have noticed—but that’s usually just an actual door or people talking somewhere nearby. The building carries sound weirdly, too, especially near the stairwells. Sorry, I’m going off track. But no, I don’t think I’m seeing or hearing things that aren’t there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “peak within minutes” sounds very specific, and I don’t know if I’ve been timing it like that.\n\nThere are definitely moments where fear comes on really fast, like my body reacts before I even understand what’s happening. But then I get caught up in trying to figure out if it was the noise, or where I was standing, or whether I’d had coffee that morning—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t know how to tell if it counts as reaching a peak. It feels intense quickly, but I haven’t really measured it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an overwhelming fear that came on very suddenly?\n\nPatient: Yes—well, I think so. It happens really suddenly if there’s a loud noise, like a door banging shut or one of those carts rattling in the hallway. I jump so hard it actually hurts in my chest.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed, because it’s not like anything terrible happened, it’s just a door, you know? But my body doesn’t seem to know that. I’ll be standing there shaking, trying to act normal, and it takes me several minutes before I can get back to whatever I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the sudden fear you feel in response to loud noises, like doors banging or carts rattling, is the same kind of fear you experience when walking across campus?\n\nPatient: Not exactly. The loud noises are more like—my whole body just reacts, like I’ve been startled.\n\nWalking across campus is different, and honestly it scares me more in a weird way. It’s like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m there but not fully in my own body. The buildings and the students around me look… slightly unreal, I guess. Like the campus is normal, people are just going to class, and I’m the one who’s somehow separated from it.\n\nSorry, I know that sounds odd. It’s not just fear like “a door slammed.” It’s more that I feel disconnected, and then that makes me scared.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an overwhelming fear or discomfort that lasted for a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s usually short, but it feels huge when it happens.\n\nLike if a door bangs shut or one of those carts rattles in the hallway, I jump so hard my chest actually hurts. And then I’m shaking, trying to pretend I’m fine, which is ridiculous because everyone else is just walking around like nothing happened. I start thinking about whether they noticed, or whether I look strange, and then I’m not even doing the thing I was supposed to be doing anymore.\n\nIt takes several minutes before I can calm down enough to get back to what I was doing. So… yes, it doesn’t last forever, but in the moment it feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes of intense fear you experience in response to loud noises could be described as panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them panic attacks, exactly. I mean, I hear “panic attack” and I think of something bigger or… I don’t know, something you see in movies.\n\nWhat happens to me is more like my body overreacts before I even understand what happened. If a door bangs or one of those carts rattles in the hallway, I jump so hard my chest hurts, and then I’m shaking for several minutes afterward. I’ll try to go back to what I was doing, but it’s like I have to wait for my body to catch up.\n\nSo maybe? I’m not sure if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of intense fear you experience in response to loud noises happen to you frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes… pretty frequently, I guess. I mean, not every single second, but if I’m in the hallway and a door bangs or one of those carts rattles past, it happens again.\n\nI jump so hard my chest hurts, and then I’m standing there trying to act normal while I’m still shaking. It takes me several minutes before I can really get back to whatever I was doing.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s just a normal hallway noise, you know? People are always moving things around, doors closing, all of that. But my body reacts like it’s something much worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body or the world around you is more distressing to you than the fear you experience in response to loud noises?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. The loud noises scare me, definitely, but that other feeling is worse in a different way.\n\nLike when I’m walking across campus, and I should just be going from one building to another, it suddenly feels like I’m watching everything through glass. The students, the buildings, even my own hands or feet moving—it’s like I’m there but not fully in my own body. And then I start looking around thinking, why does everything seem slightly unreal?\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly about the hallway noises. It’s just… the noise makes me jump, but that disconnected feeling makes me feel like something is really wrong with me.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, is it just what you see that feels unreal, or do sounds or other sensations also feel unreal to you?\n\nPatient: It’s not only what I see, I don’t think. The visual part is the easiest to explain, like everything is behind glass when I’m walking across campus—the buildings, the students passing by, even me moving through it.\n\nBut it’s more like the whole moment feels unreal. Like I’m there, but not fully in my body. I’ll be walking to class or across the quad, and part of me is thinking about something normal, like whether I remembered my notes, and then suddenly everything feels… off. Slightly distant.\n\nThe sounds are there, I guess, but they feel like they’re happening on the other side of that glass too. It scares me because I know I’m supposed to feel present, and I don’t.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, do the sounds around you feel normal?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean… I still hear people talking or footsteps or whatever’s going on around campus, but when that glass feeling happens, it all feels kind of far away, like it’s part of a scene I’m watching instead of something I’m actually in.\n\nWhat scares me most is that I’m walking across campus and the buildings and students look slightly unreal, and even I don’t feel fully in my own body. Then the sounds sort of get pulled into that too, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, do you also feel disconnected from your physical sensations, like touch or smell?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least with my body. It’s hard to separate it out into touch or smell exactly, because when it happens I’m usually just trying to get across campus and not look strange, you know?\n\nBut what scares me most is that I feel like I’m watching everything through glass. Like the buildings and students around me are slightly unreal, and I’m moving through it, but I’m not fully in my own body. I’ll notice my feet walking or my hands holding my bag, but it feels distant somehow.\n\nSmell… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to that part. It’s more the feeling that I’m there but not really there.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you're watching everything through glass, are your emotions also feeling distant or unreal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they do, sort of. It’s hard to explain because I’m usually more focused on how scary the whole thing feels.\n\nWhen I’m walking across campus and it’s like I’m watching everything through glass, the buildings and students look a little unreal, and I don’t feel fully in my own body. And then even my feelings don’t feel quite normal, like they’re happening from farther away.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, I should feel more panic right now, or I should feel more connected to what’s happening, but instead I’m just… floating through it. And then I start worrying that people can tell, which is probably silly because everyone’s rushing to class, and I used to notice that more when I was teaching sections too, how nobody really looks at anyone closely. But in the moment it feels really frightening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're watching everything through glass all the time when you're on campus, or are there moments when it stops and you feel fully present?\n\nPatient: No, not all the time. I mean, campus can set it off, especially when I’m walking between buildings and there are students everywhere, and suddenly it’s that… glass feeling again.\n\nBut there are moments when it eases up. Like if I stop focusing on it, or if I get into a classroom or someone talks to me directly, sometimes I feel more here again. It’s just when it happens, it feels so convincing. The buildings and people look slightly unreal, and I feel like I’m moving through it without being fully in my body.\n\nSorry, I know I keep circling back to that, but that’s the part that scares me most.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel fully connected to your body and surroundings when you're on campus?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like every second on campus is like that.\n\nIf I’m in a room or focused on something specific, I can feel more normal, more… in myself. But when I’m walking across campus, especially with the buildings and students all around, that’s when it hits me. It feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m there but not quite in my body, and the whole place looks slightly unreal.\n\nIt’s strange because I know it’s campus, I know where I am. I used to be so used to it, with teaching and running between offices, and now just crossing it can feel like I’m in some scene I’m observing instead of living. That’s the part that really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these feelings of being disconnected from your body and the world around you have been happening for more than 2 months?\n\nPatient: No, not more than two months. It’s only been since the lighting rig collapsed during the showcase… so, about six days now.\n\nIt feels longer, honestly, because I keep replaying little pieces of it and campus feels different, like places I used to just walk through without thinking. I remember before all this I was worried about grading and whether I’d left my office keys somewhere, normal stupid stuff, and now I’m counting days like that. But no, it hasn’t been months. Just these past six days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "duration_verified": "symptoms have been present for approximately six days, which is less than the typical duration required for a diagnosis of panic disorder, but the patient's symptoms are severe and interfere with daily life",
    "functional_impairment": true,
